Running speed is derived from `dataset.running_speed`, specifically the `speed` and `timestamps` columns.\n\nii.\n```python\nrun = ed['running_speed']\nrts, rsp = run['timestamps'].values, run['speed'].values\n```\n\niii. The SDK's `running_speed` attribute provides the standard locomotion measurement.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Running speed*?\n\ni. Running speed is linearly interpolated (after removing NaN values) to the resampled bin centers, then discretized into 5 percentile-based bins. Bin edges are set to `-inf` and `inf` at the extremes. NaN values are mapped to the middle bin.\n\nii.\n```python\nvm = ~np.isnan(rsp)\nri = interpolate.interp1d(rts[vm], rsp[vm], 'linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)(bc)\nrb = dig(ri, rbe)\n\ndef pct_bins(v, n=5):\n    e = np.percentile(v2, np.linspace(0, 100, n+1))\n    e[0] = -np.inf; e[-1] = np.inf\n    return e\n\ndef dig(v, e):\n    b = np.clip(np.digitize(v, e) - 1, 0, len(e) - 2)\n    b[np.isnan(v)] = (len(e) - 1) // 2\n    return b.astype(np.int64)\n```\n\niii. Percentile-based binning ensures roughly equal class counts. Setting edge bins to +/-inf ensures all values are captured. NaN mapped to middle bin rather than bin 0.\n\n## 5-c. How is `output` *Running speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Discretized into 5 equal percentile bins (0th, 20th, 40th, 60th, 80th, 100th percentiles). Edges are set to -inf/inf.\n\nii. See 5-b.\n\niii. Percentile-based binning ensures balanced classes.\n\n## 5-d. How is `output` *Running speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Running speed is interpolated to the same bin centers used for neural data, then extracted per-trial using the same time bin indices.\n\nii.\n```python\nri = interpolate.interp1d(rts[vm], rsp[vm], 'linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)(bc)\n```\n\niii. Same time base ensures alignment.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Pupil diameter* derived from?\n\ni. Pupil diameter is derived from `dataset.eye_tracking`, using the `pupil_area` column (NOT pupil_width).\n\nii.\n```python\nets, pa = eye['timestamps'].values, eye['pupil_area'].values\n```\n\niii. From CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 10: \"Fixed pupil area collection in h5py (use area key, not width*height)\". The AI chose `pupil_area` as the measure.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Pupil diameter*?\n\ni. Pupil area is linearly interpolated (after removing NaN values) to the resampled bin centers, then discretized into 5 percentile-based bins. NaN values from interpolation are mapped to the middle bin.\n\nii.\n```python\nvm2 = ~np.isnan(pa)\npi = interpolate.interp1d(ets[vm2], pa[vm2], 'linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)(bc)\npb = dig(pi, pbe)\n```\n\niii. Same approach as running speed. NaN values are removed before interpolation (rather than using `likely_blink` flag for blink filtering).\n\n## 6-c. How is `output` *Pupil diameter* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Same as running speed — 5 equal percentile bins with -inf/inf edges. NaN mapped to middle bin.\n\nii. See 6-b and `pct_bins`/`dig` functions.\n\niii. Same percentile-based approach as running speed.\n\n## 6-d. How is `output` *Pupil diameter* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Pupil area is interpolated to the same bin centers used for neural data.\n\nii. Same as running speed alignment.\n\niii. Same time base ensures alignment.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Trial outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Trial outcome is derived from the boolean columns `hit`, `miss`, `false_alarm`, and `correct_reject` in the trials table. A priority-based check maps each trial to one of these four categories.\n\nii.\n```python\noc = 0 if t['hit'] else (1 if t['miss'] else (2 if t['false_alarm'] else 3))\n```\n\niii. These four columns are the SDK's canonical trial outcome labels, mutually exclusive for valid (non-aborted, non-auto-rewarded) trials.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Trial outcome*?\n\ni. Trial outcome is mapped to an integer code (0=hit, 1=miss, 2=false_alarm, 3=correct_reject) and broadcast as a constant across all time bins in the trial.\n\nii.\n```python\noc = 0 if t['hit'] else (1 if t['miss'] else (2 if t['false_alarm'] else 3))\n...\notl.append(np.stack([ib[ti], cb[ti], rb[ti], pb[ti], np.full(len(ti), oc, dtype=np.int64)]))\n```\n\niii. The mapping order is fixed. The outcome is per-trial, so it is replicated across all time bins.\n\n## 8. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1.
